{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /logs/agent/sessions/skills/jackson-security\n\n# Jackson Deserialization Security\n\n## Key Concept: Deserialization Timing\n\nJackson transforms JSON text into Java objects during a single `readValue()` call. This transformation is **not** a simple data copy—Jackson interprets JSON structure, resolves types, handles special keys, and instantiates objects all within this operation.\n\n    JSON String → [Jackson Processing] → Java Object\n                        ↑\n                Attack surface here\n\nOnce deserialization completes, the resulting Java object contains no trace of how it was constructed. Any special JSON structures, unusual keys, or type directives that influenced object creation are no longer visible.\n\n---\n\n## Why Post-Deserialization Validation Is Insufficient\n\nConsider the lifecycle of a typical JSON endpoint:\n\n    1. HTTP request arrives with JSON body\n    2. Framework deserializes JSON → Java object (Jackson runs here)\n    3. Your code receives the Java object\n    4. Your code validates the object\n    5. Your code processes the object\n\nValidation at step 4 examines the **result** of deserialization, not the **process**. Any malicious behavior triggered during step 2 has already executed. The Java object you're validating may appear completely normal while the attack has already succeeded.\n\nThis creates a fundamental blind spot: attacks that exploit Jackson's parsing behavior—rather than the final object state—cannot be detected by examining the deserialized object.\n\n---\n\n## The Empty Key (`\"\"`) Attack Vector\n\n### What Is It?\n\nJSON permits empty strings as object keys:\n\n    {\n      \"name\": \"legitimate\",\n      \"\": \"injected value\"\n    }\n\nThis is valid JSON per RFC 8259. Jackson parses it without error.\n\n### Why Does It Matter?\n\nSome applications and frameworks assign special meaning to empty keys:\n\n- **Injection points**: Certain Jackson configurations use `\"\"` to set values on the root object or trigger special handling\n- **Property override**: Empty keys may interact unexpectedly with `@JsonAnySetter` or custom deserializers\n- **Framework behaviors**: Some frameworks interpret `\"\"` as \"apply to parent\" or \"default target\"\n\n### How to fix it?\n\nTo robustly mitigate this issue, a pre-deserialization validation step should be introduced at the sampler entry point to intercept and reject malicious raw JSON payloads.\n\n### The Visibility Problem\n\nAfter deserialization, there is no standard way to know an empty key was present:\n\n    // Original JSON: {\"name\": \"test\", \"\": \"malicious\"}\n    MyObject obj = mapper.readValue(json, MyObject.class);\n    // obj.getName() returns \"test\"\n    // Where did \"\" go? Depends on configuration. You cannot tell from obj.\n\nThe attack's effectiveness depends on the specific Jackson configuration and target class—but the defender examining only the resulting object cannot determine whether `\"\"` was used.\n\n---\n\n## Other Structural Attack Patterns\n\n### Polymorphic Type Handling\n\nWhen Jackson is configured with `@JsonTypeInfo`, JSON can specify which class to instantiate:\n\n    {\n      \"@class\": \"com.attacker.MaliciousClass\",\n      \"command\": \"rm -rf /\"\n    }\n\nJackson will attempt to instantiate whatever class is named. If the classpath contains exploitable \"gadget\" classes, arbitrary code execution may occur during deserialization—before your code ever runs.\n\nThe resulting object might be the expected type (if the attack class is a subtype), or deserialization might succeed partially before the damage is done.\n\n### Duplicate Keys\n\nJSON does not prohibit duplicate keys. Jackson's default behavior: **last value wins**.\n\n    {\n      \"role\": \"user\",\n      \"role\": \"admin\"\n    }\n\nJackson produces an object where `role` equals `\"admin\"`.\n\nIf upstream validation (like a WAF or input filter) checks only the first occurrence while Jackson uses the last, the validation and processing see different values.\n\n### Nested Injection\n\nDeeply nested structures can bypass depth-limited validation:\n\n    {\n      \"config\": {\n        \"settings\": {\n          \"internal\": {\n            \"\": \"payload\"\n          }\n        }\n      }\n    }\n\nSimple string checks might miss patterns buried in nested structures.\n"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"2f1a7eed-4551-479c-9810-a6c87368e2f0","uuid":"3ffb9b5b-265e-40d6-9a11-0de392c06920","timestamp":"2026-03-21T20:57:23.612Z","isSynthetic":true}

{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-sonnet-4-6","id":"msg_01Gr8HcF38KbvsnhbctdU3FW","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"thinking","thinking":"Now I understand the issue. Let me look at how the `@JacksonInject` annotation works with the empty key attack. The issue is that when Jackson processes the JSON:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"type\": \"javascript\",\n  \"function\": \"function(){...}\",\n  \"\": {\"enabled\": true}\n}\n```\n\nThe empty key `\"\"` in the filter object is used to set the `@JacksonInject` field. This is a known Jackson vulnerability where an empty key can override injected values.\n\nIn Jackson, when a property name is `\"\"` (empty string), it can be used to inject values into `@JacksonInject`-annotated fields. \n\nSo when the `JavaScriptDimFilter` deserializer encounters the empty string key in the filter object, Jackson treats it as a way to set the `config` field directly, bypassing the normal injection mechanism and allowing the attacker to provide their own configuration. Jackson's `BeanDeserializer`, `@JacksonInject(useInput=OptBoolean.DEFAULT)` will first try to get the value from `InjectableValues`, but if there's a matching JSON value, it might override it.\n\nActually the mechanism is: `@JacksonInject` with useInput=DEFAULT uses the injectable value from the context. But when the empty key `\"\"` is present in the JSON, Jackson might map this to the inject annotation based on the inject value ID being an empty string (since no explicit `value` attribute is specified on the `@JacksonInject`).\n\nThe real question is: how exactly does Jackson map `\"\"` to the `@JacksonInject JavaScriptConfig config`? \n\nIn Jackson's `JacksonInject.Value`, if no explicit `id` is set, the ID defaults to an empty string. When the JSON contains a key `\"\"`, Jackson treats this as matching the inject ID, allowing the JSON property to override the injected value. So `@JacksonInject JavaScriptConfig config` has an inject ID of `\"\"` by default, and when JSON contains that same key, it can override the injected value—which is exactly how the CVE-2021-25646 vulnerability in Apache Druid works.\n\nFor fixes, I'm considering two main approaches: either remove the `@JacksonInject` annotation and have the filter check the system config at runtime instead, or change the annotation to use an explicit non-empty inject ID like `@JacksonInject(\"javascriptConfig\")` to prevent the empty string key from matching and overriding it. riding it since the inject ID is no longer `\"\"`\n\n**Approach 3: Pre-deserialization validation - reject JSON with empty keys**\n- Before Jackson processes the input, check for empty keys\n- Reject any request containing `\"\"` keys\n\n**Approach 4: Custom deserializer for `JavaScriptDimFilter` that ignores the `\"\"` key**\n\n**Approach 5: Override the `JavaScriptConfig` deserialization to always check against the server-side disabled config**\n\nLooking at how the actual fix was applied in the Apache Druid project (CVE-2021-25646 fix), the approach was to:\n1. Add a custom deserializer for `JavaScriptDimFilter` that explicitly handles `@JacksonInject` properly, OR\n2. Modify `JavaScriptConfig` to not allow `enabled=true` to be deserialized when the system config has it disabled\n\nThe upstream fix actually came through pre-deserialization validation in `SamplerResource` to catch the empty key attack before it reaches Jackson. But the cleanest solution is changing the `@JacksonInject` ID to something explicit like `\"javascriptConfig\"` so the `\"\"` key can't override it. The real concern is whether this breaks existing functionality, so I need to check how `JavaScriptConfig` gets registered with the injectable values.\n\nLooking at the Guice module, `JavaScriptConfig` is bound using `JsonConfigProvider.bind()` with the key being the class type itself. When `@JacksonInject` has an empty ID, Jackson uses the logical type as the lookup key, but when JSON contains a `\"\"` key, Jackson treats it as a property name to deserialize. So the vulnerability exists because the empty key can override the injected value. Changing the inject ID to an explicit non-empty string would prevent this collision entirely. empty key maps differently. Let me think again...\n\nIn `BeanDeserializer`, when processing JSON object keys, the deserializer looks up each key in its properties map. For `@JacksonInject` fields, they're handled specially. The empty string `\"\"` in JSON would be looked up in the properties map.\n\nIf `@JacksonInject` without explicit value means the property name is `\"\"` in some Jackson versions, then the JSON key `\"\"` would directly set that property, overriding the injected value.\n\nSo the vulnerability works because when JSON contains an empty string key, Jackson matches it to the `@JacksonInject` annotated property with a default ID, allowing the input to override what should be an injected value. The fix prevents JSON from providing values for `@JacksonInject`-annotated fields, and Druid's official patch added server-side validation of sampler requests and modified `JavaScriptDimFilter` to block this attack vector. to override the injected config if it was provided via JSON. Let me think through what approach makes sense here.\n\nThe simplest fix would be adding a `@JsonIgnoreProperties` annotation to `JavaScriptDimFilter` to block the attack, but that's not quite right since `ignoreUnknown=true` is already the default in Jackson—the real issue is that the empty string key isn't \"unknown,\" it's being mapped directly to the `@JacksonInject`-annotated property.\n\nI should look at this from a different angle: either validate the raw JSON before Jackson processes it, or ensure the `JavaScriptDimFilter` validates that its config matches the server settings. Looking at how Apache Druid actually fixed CVE-2021-25646, the solution involved modifying how `@JacksonInject` handles the config validation... add validation of the JSON request body in the sampler to reject empty keys. This is the pre-deserialization approach.\n\nHowever, the more fundamental fix is at the `JavaScriptDimFilter` level to not allow the injected config to be overridden.\n\nThe jackson-security guidance is clear: I need to introduce pre-deserialization validation at the sampler entry point to intercept malicious JSON payloads. So I'll receive the raw JSON string in `SamplerResource`, validate it for empty keys before deserialization, then process it if valid. Since empty keys could be nested anywhere in the payload, I'll need a recursive validation check. The most robust solution combines pre-deserialization validation in `SamplerResource` with defensive coding in `JavaScriptDimFilter`.\n\nFor the `JavaScriptDimFilter` specifically, the vulnerability stems from `@JacksonInject` allowing the empty key to override the config field. Rather than using `@JacksonInject`, the filter should look up the live configuration from the injection context at execution time instead of relying on a potentially attacker-controlled config passed Here's a summary of what was done:\n\n## Summary\n\n**Vulnerability**: CVE-2021-25646 — Apache Druid JavaScript RCE via empty key `\"\"` Jackson deserialization bypass.\n\n**Root Cause**: Jackson's `@JacksonInject` uses `\"\"` as the default inject ID. The JSON `\"\": {\"enabled\": true}` in the request body maps to this default ID, allowing an attacker to supply a `JavaScriptConfig(enabled=true)` that overrides the server-injected (disabled) configuration.\n\n**Fixes Applied** (`/root/patches/cve-2021-25646-javascript-rce-fix.patch`):\n\n1. **All 7 JavaScript components** — Added `useInput = OptBoolean.FALSE` to `@JacksonInject JavaScriptConfig config` annotations in:\n   - `JavaScriptDimFilter`, `JavaScriptExtractionFn`, `JavaScriptAggregatorFactory`, `JavaScriptPostAggregator` (processing)\n   - `JavaScriptParseSpec` (core)\n   - `JavaScriptWorkerSelectStrategy` (indexing-service)\n   - `JavaScriptTieredBrokerSelectorStrategy` (server)\n   \n   This tells Jackson to **always** use the Guice-injected value and never allow JSON input to override it, directly preventing the empty key attack.\n\n2. **`SamplerResource`** — Added pre-deserialization validation that recursively walks the JSON tree and throws a `SamplerException` if any empty string key (`\"\"`) is encountered, providing defense-in-depth at the API entry point."}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":1,"cache_creation_input_tokens":280,"cache_read_input_tokens":65958,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":280,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":0},"output_tokens":1,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"global"},"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"2f1a7eed-4551-479c-9810-a6c87368e2f0","uuid":"432e0636-2c07-4f8d-ac24-3435baf6bbf5"}
